# 🎯 The Virtuous Reasoner: Intellectual Character as Cognitive Infrastructure

## Report 05 — The Architecture of Excellent Thinking

---

## Phase I: The Premise 🎯

> [!question]
> **The Core Question**
> If the intellectual virtues — [[Intellectual-Humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], [[Intellectual-Empathy]], [[Intellectual-Perseverance]], [[Intellectual-Integrity]] — are genuinely valuable for thinking well, what kind of value do they have? Are they moral achievements that happen to improve cognition, or are they cognitive achievements that happen to have moral dimensions? And does the distinction matter for how we cultivate them?

The answer to this question has been obscured by a persistent mismatch in vocabulary. Philosophers working in the tradition of [[Virtue-Epistemology]] have long argued that intellectual virtues are central to the epistemic life, to the attainment of knowledge, to the formation of justified belief. Cognitive scientists studying [[Motivated-Reasoning]], [[Cognitive-Biases]], and [[Dual-Process-Theory]] have simultaneously built a meticulous catalogue of the ways in which human reasoning goes systematically wrong — confirmation bias, myside bias, the illusion of explanatory depth, the Dunning-Kruger phenomenon, identity-protective cognition. These two literatures have grown up largely in parallel, sharing a subject matter — human reasoning and its failures — while rarely making direct contact.

The synthesis that this report undertakes begins by noticing that this parallel development is not a coincidence to be explained but a structural homology to be exploited. When cognitive scientists describe [[Confirmation-Bias]], they are describing the systematic failure that [[Intellectual-Humility]] exists to correct. When they describe [[Epistemic-Cowardice]] — the tendency to give vague or uncommitted answers to avoid social friction — they are describing the failure that [[Intellectual-Courage]] exists to correct. When they describe in-group favouritism and the inability to represent opposing arguments in their strongest form, they are describing the failure that [[Intellectual-Empathy]] exists to correct. The intellectual virtues, it turns out, are not a random list of admirable qualities compiled by ancient moralists. They are a corrective architecture — a set of targeted interventions in the human reasoning system, each calibrated to address a specific, empirically confirmed failure mode.

This is the central argument of this report, and it departs significantly from how the intellectual virtues are usually presented. Standard treatments of intellectual virtue, whether in philosophy or in educational psychology, tend to present virtues as intrinsically valuable qualities of good persons — traits that a morally excellent inquirer will possess, whose practical consequences include better reasoning. On this picture, the virtues are morally primary and epistemically derivative. The synthesis here inverts this framing. The virtues are epistemically primary and morally derivative. They matter first and most urgently because human cognition is systematically unreliable in precisely the ways that the virtues correct — and developing them is, in the most practical sense, doing cognitive engineering on the hardware of human reasoning.

This departure from standard treatments is not merely a framing preference. It has significant practical implications. If intellectual virtues are moral qualities, we cultivate them through moral habituation, character education, exemplar modeling, and ethical reflection. If they are cognitive tools, we cultivate them the way we cultivate any cognitive tool: through targeted practice, feedback loops, deliberate effort in conditions of progressive difficulty, and attention to the specific failures we are trying to correct. In practice, these approaches converge — but understanding which one is primary clarifies what to do first and why.

The synthesis draws on three intellectual traditions that have rarely been brought into direct conversation: [[Virtue-Epistemology]] in its responsibilist form (which treats virtues as good intellectual character traits, following Zagzebski, Baehr, and Roberts and Wood), [[Stoic-Virtue-Ethics]] (which treats virtue as a form of knowledge and understands vice as a cognitive failure — an error in judgment about what matters), and contemporary cognitive science on [[Motivated-Reasoning]] and [[Cognitive-Biases]] (which has generated an increasingly precise taxonomy of the ways human reasoning systematically deviates from truth-conducive norms). The first tradition tells us what the virtues are. The second tells us why virtue is a cognitive state rather than merely a moral one. The third tells us which failures the virtues are designed to correct and gives us empirical access to those failures that ancient virtue theory did not have.

Together, these three traditions produce an emergent insight that neither could produce alone: that the cultivation of intellectual character is not merely morally advisable but cognitively necessary, and that the specific content of each virtue is determined not by moral tradition but by the architecture of human reasoning and its characteristic failure modes.

> [!principle-point]
> **The Framing Shift That Changes Everything**
> The standard framing asks: "What kind of person should a good thinker be?" The synthesis framing asks: "What cognitive failures does systematic good thinking require us to correct?" These questions point to the same answer — the intellectual virtues — but the second framing reveals why that answer has the specific content it does, makes the virtues practically actionable, and connects the ancient philosophical project of cultivating good intellectual character to the modern scientific project of understanding and improving human reasoning.

---

## Phase II: The Exposition 📖

### The Landscape of Virtue Epistemology: Two Traditions in Necessary Tension

[[Virtue-Epistemology]] as a contemporary philosophical movement emerged most clearly from the work of Ernest Sosa in the late 1970s and early 1980s, though its roots in [[Aristotle]]'s account of intellectual virtues run considerably deeper. The field quickly divided into two distinct camps that have remained in productive tension ever since.

The first camp, known as virtue reliabilism and associated primarily with Sosa and Alvin Goldman, treats intellectual virtues as stable, reliable, truth-conducive cognitive faculties. On this account, paradigm virtues include perception, memory, and introspection — the basic cognitive processes that reliably produce true beliefs when functioning normally. What makes these faculties virtues is not anything about the agent's character or intention but their reliable connection to truth. A person cannot choose whether their visual perception is a virtue; it either reliably produces accurate perceptions or it does not. Virtue reliabilism is primarily concerned with constructing virtue-based accounts of knowledge and justified belief, and it represents the externalist wing of virtue epistemology — the account according to which what makes a belief justified depends on factors the agent need not be aware of.

The second camp, virtue responsibilism, is the tradition most directly relevant to the synthesis this report is building. Virtue responsibilists — following Lorraine Code, Linda Zagzebski, Jason Baehr, and Roberts and Wood — conceive of intellectual virtues as good intellectual character traits, qualities of character that make someone a responsible and reliable knower and inquirer. These are the traits that concern us here: [[Intellectual-Humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], [[Intellectual-Empathy]], [[Intellectual-Perseverance]], [[Intellectual-Integrity]], open-mindedness, intellectual carefulness, and thoroughness. Unlike the perceptual and mnemonic faculties that concern virtue reliabilists, these traits are genuinely character traits in the Aristotelian sense — acquired through practice, reinforced through habituation, responsive to reasons, and capable of being exercised with varying degrees of excellence. A person can choose, at least in principle, to cultivate intellectual humility, and the failure to do so carries a kind of moral and epistemic responsibility.

Zagzebski's account is particularly important for the synthesis because she argues that knowledge should be understood as true belief arising from acts of intellectual virtue. On her account, the virtues are not merely causally connected to knowledge — it is not simply that virtuous people happen to come to know things. Rather, the epistemic value of beliefs is partly constituted by the intellectual character from which they arise. A true belief formed through intellectual courage — through willingness to follow evidence where it leads despite social or professional pressure to do otherwise — is epistemically better than a true belief formed by intellectual luck, even if both propositions are equally true. This is a striking and controversial claim, but it points at something real: the quality of one's reasoning process matters to the epistemic status of one's conclusions, not just to their truth value.

> [!summary]
> **Conceptual Materials in Hand (Part 1)**
> The virtue responsibilist tradition gives us a set of intellectual character traits — intellectual humility, courage, empathy, perseverance, integrity — that it characterizes as constitutive of good epistemic agency. Zagzebski's account connects these traits to the value of knowledge itself. What this tradition does not provide is a systematic account of why the virtues have the specific content they do — why these particular traits and not others. For that, we need to cross-reference with the Stoics and with cognitive science.

### Stoic Virtue as Cognitive Architecture: The Ancient Precedent

The Stoic account of virtue is, at first encounter, radically different from the contemporary virtue epistemological account. The Stoics held that virtue is a form of knowledge — specifically, knowledge of what is good, bad, and indifferent (ta adiaphora). This is not knowledge in a thin, propositional sense, as if one could simply be told that health is merely a preferred indifferent and thereby acquire the relevant virtue. The Stoic understanding of virtue-as-knowledge is thick and dispositional: virtue is a stable, integrated cognitive and affective state in which one's judgments, desires, and actions are consistently aligned with correct understanding of value. As Chris Gill explains, [[Stoicism]] insists that these forms of knowledge shape the personality as a whole, including the emotions, because in Stoic psychological theory, beliefs and reasoning directly shape how we feel and desire. Virtue, on this account, is not a moral achievement bolted onto a reasoning apparatus — it is a condition of the reasoning apparatus itself.

The Stoic concept of *prohairesis* — the faculty of intentional choice, roughly the will or rational agency — is the cognitive center of the Stoic ethical system. Epictetus, in the *Discourses*, argues that *prohairesis* is the only thing that is truly "up to us" (eph' hemin), the only domain in which we are genuinely free and responsible. What this means, in cognitive terms, is that the Stoics located both virtue and freedom at the level of assent — at the point at which the rational faculty either endorses or withholds endorsement from an impression (phantasia). The virtuous person is one who has trained their assent-giving capacity to operate with consistent, reliable accuracy. The vicious person is one whose assent-giving is corrupted by false beliefs, habitual errors, or emotional disturbances — what the Stoics called *pathe*, the passions that arise from incorrect judgments about value.

What is crucial for the synthesis is the Stoic claim that passions — anger, fear, craving, excessive grief — are not irrational feelings that overwhelm reason from outside. They are cognitive errors. They are false judgments, and their disturbing character follows directly from their falsity. Fear of death, for instance, is not an irrational emotion that overcomes rational judgment; it is the judgment that death is bad, which is false on the Stoic account (death being a mere indifferent), plus the affective signature that attaches to judgments about genuine harm. Eliminating inappropriate fear is not a matter of suppressing feelings but of correcting cognitive errors. The Stoics were, in this sense, the original cognitive therapists — a historical connection that Aaron Beck and Albert Ellis explicitly acknowledged in developing [[Cognitive-Behavioral-Therapy]], which is philosophically Stoic in its basic structure.

The connection to intellectual virtue becomes visible when we notice that Stoic *passions* are motivationally distorting cognitive states, and that the principal intellectual vices — intellectual arrogance, intellectual cowardice, closed-mindedness — are similarly motivated states, states in which reasoning is driven by desires unrelated to truth (the desire to protect one's reputation, the desire to avoid conflict, the desire to maintain a comfortable worldview). [[Intellectual-Courage]], on the Stoic analysis, is the cognitive discipline of allowing only truth-relevant considerations to govern one's assent. [[Intellectual-Integrity]] is the cognitive discipline of ensuring consistency between what one's evidence supports and what one is willing to assert. These are not moral achievements added on top of an independent reasoning process; they are the conditions under which reasoning operates without contamination by non-truth-directed motivations.

### The Bias Architecture: What Cognitive Science Tells Us About Systematic Failure

The third tradition contributing to this synthesis is the most empirically rich and the most recent. Beginning with Kahneman and Tversky's heuristics and biases research program in the 1970s and extending through the development of [[Dual-Process-Theory]] and the subsequent research on [[Motivated-Reasoning]], cognitive science has constructed a detailed and partially systematic account of how human reasoning deviates from truth-conducive norms.

The key distinction for this synthesis is between cold cognitive errors — failures that arise from the inherent limitations of human cognition (working memory constraints, the availability heuristic, anchoring effects) — and hot cognitive errors, which arise when reasoning is motivated by goals other than truth-finding. [[Motivated-Reasoning]], as defined by Ziva Kunda's seminal 1990 account, is reasoning that is influenced by motivational factors — the desire to reach a particular conclusion — in ways that systematically bias the process. Kunda's careful empirical analysis showed that motivated reasoners are not simply ignoring contrary evidence; they are engaging in a sophisticated search for reasons to believe what they want to believe, drawing on their genuine cognitive capacities to construct a case for a predetermined conclusion. Motivated reasoning is intelligent reasoning in service of non-epistemic goals.

The subsequent research has been enormously productive. Hugo Mercier and Dan Sperber's argumentative theory of reasoning, first fully articulated in 2011, proposes that human reasoning evolved primarily to produce arguments for social persuasion rather than to track truth — which elegantly explains why motivated reasoning is so pervasive and so cognitively sophisticated. The myside bias literature has documented with considerable precision the tendency to evaluate arguments that support one's prior beliefs more favorably than arguments that challenge them. The research on identity-protective cognition has shown that for beliefs closely tied to group membership and social identity, the relationship between intelligence and accurate belief formation can actually be negative: more intelligent people are better at constructing rationalizations, so they arrive at motivated conclusions with greater sophistication and apparent justification.

[[Dual-Process-Theory]] provides the mechanistic account that underlies all of these phenomena. System 1 thinking is fast, automatic, associative, and heavily influenced by prior beliefs, emotional states, and social context. System 2 thinking is slow, deliberate, rule-governed, and capable of overriding System 1 outputs — but only when it is actively engaged, which requires effort, and only when it is not itself captured by motivated reasoning, which is the subtler and more dangerous failure mode. The intellectual virtues, on the analysis this report is building, function primarily as mechanisms for ensuring that System 2 is engaged when it should be and that, when engaged, it is governed by truth-directed rather than motivation-directed goals.

> [!summary]
> **Conceptual Materials in Hand (Part 2)**
> The three traditions now in view give us: (1) a normative account of what intellectual virtues are and why they matter for knowledge (virtue epistemology); (2) a claim that virtue is itself a cognitive state in which reasoning is governed by truth-directed rather than motivation-directed processes (Stoicism); and (3) a detailed empirical taxonomy of the motivated reasoning failures that corrupted cognition produces (cognitive bias science). The synthesis move is to read these three traditions against each other: the Stoic account tells us *why* virtue is cognitive, the bias science tells us *which failures* each virtue corrects, and virtue epistemology provides the normative framework that explains why correcting these failures constitutes an epistemic achievement.

---

## Phase III: The Dialectic ⚔️

### The Structural Homology: Virtue as Knowledge and Knowledge as Virtue 🔍

The first synthesis move identifies a structural homology between the Stoic account of virtue as episteme (knowledge) and the virtue epistemological account of justified belief. Both traditions converge on the same underlying insight: that the cognitive quality of a belief is not fully determined by the evidence available to the reasoner but also by the quality of the cognitive process through which the reasoner engages that evidence. Two reasoners facing identical evidence — say, evidence bearing on a contested empirical question that intersects with their prior ideological commitments — may arrive at radically different conclusions not because they have different amounts of information but because one is reasoning with intellectual honesty and the other is engaged in motivated reasoning. The epistemically superior process produces more reliable beliefs, is more responsive to counter-evidence, and generates conclusions that will prove more robust over time.

What the Stoics grasped, and what virtue epistemologists have formalized in contemporary terms, is that this quality difference is not accidental. It corresponds to a systematic difference in the cognitive state of the reasoner — a difference that is stable enough to persist across contexts, that is cultivable through practice, and that has consequences that extend far beyond any single reasoning episode. This is why both traditions use the language of character. Character traits are stable dispositions that manifest across contexts; they are not mere habits (which might be more contextually restricted) but deep cognitive orientations that shape how one approaches any problem where truth-finding is at stake.

The homology goes deeper still. The Stoic distinction between *kataleptic impressions* — cognitive impressions that meet the highest epistemic standard, arising from what is true, formed in accordance with how the object actually is, marked with the qualities that cannot belong to what is false — and ordinary impressions maps directly onto the virtue epistemologist's distinction between beliefs formed through intellectual virtue and beliefs formed through intellectual vice or simple cognitive default. In both frameworks, the highest epistemic achievement is not merely getting to a true conclusion but getting there through a process that is calibrated to the way things actually are, governed by standards that are responsive to truth rather than to desire, reputation, or comfort.

This structural homology is not just philosophically satisfying. It has practical implications. If virtue is a cognitive state — if intellectual humility, for instance, is not merely a moral disposition to acknowledge one's fallibility but is constituted by specific metacognitive processes (awareness of the limits of one's knowledge, sensitivity to the strength of counter-evidence, calibrated confidence in one's beliefs) — then we have principled guidance about how to cultivate it. We cultivate metacognitive awareness. We train sensitivity to evidence quality. We practice calibration. The virtue is, in part, a set of cognitive skills, and skills can be taught.

The empirical support for this cognitive interpretation of the virtues is increasingly strong. A 2025 study by Fischer, Kause, and Huff found robust evidence in a national US sample that intellectually humbler citizens perform better at discerning correct from incorrect interpretations of evidence on contested topics, and exhibit heightened metacognitive sensitivity — specifically, a greater ability to recognize when their reasoning is unreliable. Earlier work by Zmigrod and colleagues (2019) established that cognitive flexibility — measured with objective behavioural assessments — predicted intellectual humility, as did general intelligence, with evidence of a compensatory effect: high cognitive flexibility is particularly valuable for intellectual humility in the context of lower general intelligence, and vice versa. This finding suggests two distinct psychological pathways to intellectual humility, both mediated by cognitive rather than purely motivational mechanisms.

> [!key-claim]
> **The Cognitive Constitution of Virtue**
> The structural homology between Stoic virtue-as-episteme and virtue epistemological accounts of justified belief reveals that intellectual virtues are not moral traits that causally produce good reasoning outcomes. They are cognitive states — specifically, calibrated orientations of the assent-giving faculty — in which reasoning is constitutively governed by truth-directed rather than motivation-directed processes. The moral dimensions of intellectual virtue are real but secondary: they arise because reasoning that is calibrated to truth rather than desire tends also to be honest, fair, respectful, and epistemically responsible. But the cognitive constitution is primary.

### The Conceptual Tension: The Situationist Challenge and What It Reveals 🔥

The most serious empirical challenge to virtue theory, whether moral or intellectual, comes from situationism — the claim, associated with the social psychologists John Darley, Lee Ross, Gilbert Harman, and most rigorously with John Doris and Mark Alfano, that psychological research suggests human dispositions are far more context-sensitive than virtue theory requires. If intellectual humility is a genuine character trait, it should manifest across a wide range of situations — a person should be humble about their beliefs whether they are in a low-stakes academic conversation or a high-stakes ideological confrontation. But empirical research consistently suggests that the same person may be remarkably humble in one context and remarkably dogmatic in another. The social psychological literature on motivated reasoning makes the situationist point with particular force: when beliefs are connected to social identity, values, or reputation, intellectual humility appears to evaporate even among people who exhibit it robustly in other contexts.

Alfano's version of the challenge, directed specifically at virtue epistemology, constructs what he calls an inconsistent triad: anti-skepticism (most people have quite a lot of knowledge), epistemic situationism (most people's intellectual dispositions are not genuinely cross-situationally stable), and virtue epistemology's account of knowledge (knowledge requires beliefs that manifest intellectual virtue). If all three are true, most of our ordinary true beliefs do not count as knowledge — which is a reductio of something. Either we must accept that most of what we call knowledge is not knowledge, or we must reject epistemic situationism, or we must revise virtue epistemology's account.

This tension is real, and the synthesis does not dissolve it. Instead, it uses it — because the tension reveals something important about the structure of intellectual virtue that the standard virtue epistemological account tends to obscure. The situationist challenge presupposes that virtues are stable character traits in the sense of cross-situationally consistent behavioural dispositions. But the Stoic account suggests a different model. For the Stoics, virtue is not a disposition to behave in certain ways across contexts; it is a cultivated attentional and assent-governing practice — a trained relationship to one's own cognitive processes. What the Stoics called *askesis* (practice or training) is precisely the cultivation of stable metacognitive habits: the ability to notice when an impression is forming, to pause before giving assent, to interrogate the impression for its evidential quality, to resist the pull of motivated cognition. This is not a character trait in the sense of a stable disposition to behave in certain ways regardless of context; it is a practice that can be more or less reliably maintained, more or less robust under conditions of pressure, more or less accessible when the conditions that normally trigger motivated reasoning are present.

Alfano's own response to the situationist challenge points in a similar direction. He argues for the possibility of what he calls scaffolded or extended virtues — intellectual dispositions that are maintained not internally (as pure character traits residing in the individual) but relationally, through the epistemic environment the agent inhabits. On this account, a person can exhibit reliable intellectual humility not because they have an internally stable disposition but because they are embedded in social and informational environments that consistently prompt and reward humble epistemic behaviour. The virtue is real — it reliably produces good epistemic outcomes — but it is distributed across the person-environment system rather than residing entirely in the individual.

This resolution of the situationist tension is, I want to argue, actually more interesting than the standard virtue theory response (which is to deny that the situationist evidence is as strong as claimed). It reveals that intellectual virtue, understood correctly, is a practice — something you do rather than something you have — and that the practice is more or less robust depending on both the individual's training and the epistemic infrastructure they inhabit. This has direct implications for how intellectual character should be cultivated. It is not enough to instil good cognitive dispositions in isolation; one must also engineer the epistemic environment in ways that consistently prompt and support virtuous practice. The Stoics understood this: Epictetus's pedagogical practice was not merely about instructing students in correct principles but about creating an environment — the philosophical school itself — in which practices of careful attention, rigorous examination, and honest assent-giving could be consistently rehearsed.

The empirical literature on intellectual humility provides striking support for this contextual account. Research consistently shows that intellectual humility is most likely to be exhibited when the stakes are low and least likely when beliefs are strongly held, connected to social identity, or under threat. People who score high on self-report measures of intellectual humility do not automatically behave humbly when their core values are challenged; they require the additional resource of a metacognitive stance — what Kross and Grossmann call "wise reasoning" — that is more available in some contexts than others. A 2024 study by Beebe established that epistemic autonomy (one's own independent reasoning) without intellectual humility as a counterbalancing virtue leads to increased belief in misinformation, pseudoscience, and conspiracy theories. The virtues must function together; they are not independently sufficient, and the system fails when one component is present without its complementary corrective.

> [!key-claim]
> **The Tension That Improves the Account**
> The situationist challenge, rather than refuting virtue epistemology, improves it by forcing a more accurate characterisation of what intellectual virtues are. They are not fixed character traits guaranteed to manifest across all contexts; they are cultivated cognitive practices that are more or less robust depending on training, context, and epistemic infrastructure. This means that intellectual character development is a project without a terminus: one does not achieve intellectual humility and then possess it permanently, but rather maintains an ongoing practice of metacognitive attention that must be continuously rehearsed, especially under conditions of pressure, threat, or motivated reasoning.

### The Mutual Enrichment: How Bias Science and Virtue Theory Complete Each Other 💡

The second synthesis move identifies the mutual enrichment that occurs when virtue epistemology and cognitive bias science are held together. Each tradition has significant explanatory gaps that the other fills.

Virtue epistemology, in its standard form, provides a rich normative account of what intellectual virtues are and why they matter but offers comparatively little explanation of why the virtues have the specific content they do. Why is *intellectual humility* a virtue rather than, say, intellectual assertiveness? Why is *intellectual courage* required rather than simple intellectual consistency? Why, in the roster of intellectual virtues, do we find exactly these traits and not others? The standard answer — that these are the traits of a good intellectual character, as recognised through moral intuition and philosophical reflection — is accurate but unsatisfying. It does not tell us what it would mean to have one virtue but not another, or why the specific combination matters.

Cognitive bias science fills this gap by providing a systematic account of the specific, empirically identified failure modes in human reasoning. When we read the intellectual virtues against the bias taxonomy, the specific content of each virtue becomes intelligible as a targeted correction: it is exactly what you need to correct for the named bias, and no more and no less than that.

Consider [[Intellectual-Humility]]. The bias it corrects is *overconfidence* and its downstream consequences: confirmation bias (the tendency to seek and evaluate evidence in ways that confirm prior beliefs), the illusion of explanatory depth (the tendency to believe one understands complex phenomena more deeply than one actually does), and what Kahan calls identity-protective cognition (the tendency to reason toward conclusions that protect group membership at the expense of accuracy). Intellectual humility, on the cognitive interpretation, is the metacognitive disposition to maintain accurate awareness of the limits of one's own knowledge and the reliability of one's own cognitive processes — exactly the disposition that corrects these specific failure modes. The empirical literature confirms this mapping: the 2022 Nature Reviews Psychology review by Porter and colleagues established that intellectual humility is constituted by a metacognitive ability to recognize the limitations of one's beliefs and knowledge, and is associated with reduced cognitive bias in decision-making, including reduced political myside bias and reduced ideological polarization.

Consider [[Intellectual-Courage]]. The bias it corrects is *epistemic cowardice* — the tendency to give vague, uncommitted, or socially acceptable answers rather than accurate ones, and the related tendency to abandon well-supported positions under social pressure rather than counter-evidence. Epistemic cowardice is not a simple failure of honesty; it is a motivated cognitive process in which the desire to avoid social friction systematically distorts one's willingness to assert, defend, or even privately maintain uncomfortable conclusions. Intellectual courage is not the disposition to be contrarian; it is the disposition to allow only evidential considerations to govern whether and how strongly one asserts a position. This maps directly onto the Stoic concept of *prohairesis* — the faculty that decides what to assent to, exercised correctly when governed by truth-tracking rather than socially motivated considerations.

Consider [[Intellectual-Empathy]], which Roberts and Wood characterise as the disposition to understand and appreciate the perspective of another inquirer, particularly one who holds different beliefs. The bias it corrects is *the asymmetric evaluation of arguments* — the robust finding (Mercier and Sperber's argumentative theory predicts this) that we evaluate arguments supporting our prior beliefs with less critical scrutiny than arguments challenging them. When intellectual empathy is exercised, one attempts to represent an opposing argument in its strongest form before criticising it — what philosopher John Stuart Mill called the practice of getting to know an opponent's case at least as well as they know it themselves. The cognitive effect is to counterbalance the asymmetric evaluation bias: one who cannot represent an opposing argument sympathetically is one who has never exercised the relevant cognitive perspective-taking that would allow the opposing argument to receive fair evaluation.

Consider [[Intellectual-Perseverance]], the disposition to sustain inquiry even when it becomes effortful, confusing, or socially unrewarded. The bias it corrects is *the satisficing tendency* — the tendency of cognitive systems, under conditions of effort, to settle for the first adequate answer rather than the best available answer. System 1, operating by default, produces rapid conclusions that are good enough for most purposes; System 2 effortful deliberation is required for problems where the right answer is counterintuitive, where the available evidence is complex, or where prior beliefs create strong interference. Intellectual perseverance is the motivational infrastructure that sustains System 2 engagement past the point at which the cognitive system would normally default back to System 1. Without it, the other virtues are practically inert: intellectual humility might produce accurate calibration of uncertainty, but without perseverance, the reasoner will not continue the inquiry long enough to resolve that uncertainty.

Consider [[Intellectual-Integrity]], the disposition to ensure consistency between one's evidence and one's assertions, between one's stated reasoning and one's actual reasoning, and across one's beliefs over time. The bias it corrects is the most sophisticated of the motivated reasoning failures: the capacity for self-deception in which one believes oneself to be reasoning honestly while actually engaged in motivated cognition. The research on motivated reasoning establishes that people are not typically aware when they are engaging in it; they experience themselves as evaluating evidence carefully and arriving at well-supported conclusions. Intellectual integrity requires precisely the metacognitive monitoring capacity to notice when one's reasoning is diverging from honest inquiry — a capacity that the research on intellectual humility and metacognition suggests is itself trainable.

> [!insight]
> **The Mutual Enrichment in Full**
> Virtue epistemology gains from cognitive bias science a systematic answer to the question "why these virtues?" — because each virtue in the canonical roster corresponds to a specific, empirically confirmed failure mode in the human reasoning architecture. Cognitive bias science gains from virtue epistemology a normative framework that transforms its descriptive catalogue of biases into a prescriptive programme for cognitive improvement — not through debiasing interventions applied from outside, but through the cultivation of internal cognitive dispositions that are structurally hostile to specific biases. Neither tradition, alone, provides both the normative account and the mechanistic account that together constitute a complete theory of excellent reasoning.

### The Stoic Unity of Virtues and Contemporary Pluralism: A Resolved Tension 🔥

One further conceptual tension deserves examination. The Stoics held a distinctive and controversial doctrine: the unity of virtues. On this account, the four Stoic cardinal virtues — prudence (*phronesis*), courage (*andreia*), temperance (*sophrosyne*), and justice (*dikaiosyne*) — are not four independent traits but four aspects of a single cognitive state: complete and integrated rationality. You cannot have genuine courage without justice, because genuine courage involves knowing what is worth being courageous for, which requires just evaluations of value. You cannot have genuine prudence without temperance, because prudent judgment requires that one's reasoning not be distorted by excessive desire. The virtues are unified because they are all aspects of the same underlying cognitive achievement: reasoning that is consistently governed by correct understanding of what matters.

Contemporary virtue epistemology, by contrast, treats the intellectual virtues as plural and independently possessed. One can have substantial intellectual humility and rather limited intellectual courage; one can be deeply intellectually empathic and somewhat deficient in intellectual perseverance. This pluralism seems confirmed by empirical psychology, which treats the various intellectual virtues as distinct constructs and measures them separately.

The synthesis here does not eliminate this tension but resolves it by recognising that both accounts are correct at different levels of analysis. At the level of operational psychology — the level at which we measure, cultivate, and develop the virtues — they are genuinely distinct. One can work on intellectual humility through targeted practices without simultaneously working on intellectual courage; the practices differ, the contexts that trigger each virtue differ, and the specific cognitive processes that each involves differ. But at the level of architectural analysis — the level at which we ask what kind of cognitive system would reliably exhibit all these traits — the Stoic unity thesis reasserts itself. A reasoning system that is genuinely governed by truth-directed rather than motivation-directed processes will, in the limit, exhibit all the intellectual virtues simultaneously, because all of them are expressions of the same underlying condition: cognition that is calibrated to reality rather than to desire.

The practical implication is that intellectual character development involves both local and global work. Local work targets specific virtues in specific contexts: practising epistemic humility about one's political beliefs, building intellectual courage in professional settings, developing intellectual empathy through structured engagement with opposing arguments. But global work aims at the underlying condition from which all the virtues flow: the cultivation of a thinking orientation in which truth-seeking is genuinely primary, in which one's reasoning is systematically oriented toward accuracy rather than self-serving conclusions. The Stoic programme of *askesis* — philosophical practice as a way of life — was global work in exactly this sense.

> [!key-claim]
> **The Two-Level Account of Virtue Unity**
> The Stoic unity of virtues and contemporary virtue pluralism are both correct: at the operational level of psychological development, the virtues are distinct and can be developed somewhat independently; at the architectural level of what makes a reasoning system excellent, they are aspects of a single underlying condition. This two-level account resolves the tension and has practical implications: intellectual character development requires both targeted local work on specific virtues and global work on the underlying orientation from which all virtues flow.

---

## Phase IV: The Application 🔧

### The Corrective Architecture in Practice: Evidence from Educational Interventions

The educational application of this synthesis is one of its most practically significant dimensions. If the intellectual virtues are cognitive tools for correcting specific reasoning failures, then intellectual character education should be measurably effective at improving cognitive outcomes, not merely at improving moral character. The empirical evidence, while still developing, is encouraging.

A randomised, pretest-posttest control trial by Orona, Edelmann, and Trautwein (2023, Current Psychology) tested a novel thinking disposition intervention on undergraduate students, measuring its effects on four key intellectual virtues: curiosity, humility, integrity, and tenacity. The intervention produced improvements in intellectual virtue development, with the strongest effects mediated by the theoretically predicted pathways. Notably, the study found clear evidence for theory-inspired mediators of virtue learning — the mechanisms through which the intervention produced its effects were the ones the virtue epistemological framework predicted. This is more than a null-hypothesis significance test; it is evidence that the theoretical framework is tracking something real about how intellectual character develops.

Earlier work by Orona (2021) and Orona and Pritchard (2022) established that intellectual virtue development is responsive to educational intervention in more naturalistic academic settings as well. Jason Baehr's book *Deep in Thought* (Harvard Education Press, 2021) provides a comprehensive account of how the Intellectual Virtues Academy in Long Beach, California — a K-12 school explicitly designed around cultivating intellectual virtues — produces demonstrable improvements in students' critical thinking, metacognitive awareness, and intellectual dispositions. The school's approach maps closely onto the corrective architecture account: rather than teaching the virtues as abstract moral ideals, the curriculum engages students in practices specifically designed to develop calibrated epistemic habits — to notice and resist the pull of motivated reasoning, to maintain intellectual perseverance in the face of confusion, to represent opposing positions with genuine understanding.

Orona and Trautwein (2023, Thinking Skills and Creativity) found that thinking disposition education improves cognitive reflection — the specific cognitive capacity to override System 1 responses in favour of System 2 deliberation. This finding connects the intellectual virtue framework directly to [[Dual-Process-Theory]]: cultivating intellectual virtues appears to enhance the capacity for System 2 engagement, precisely the mechanistic account that the synthesis predicts.

> [!example]
> **The Intellectual Virtue Audit: A Practical Protocol**
>
> The corrective architecture account generates a practical self-assessment protocol. For each intellectual virtue, there is a corresponding failure mode to audit — a pattern of reasoning to look for in one's own thinking as evidence that the relevant virtue is not currently active.
>
> *Intellectual Humility Audit*: In the domain I am reasoning about, am I seeking evidence that would disconfirm my current views with the same energy I am seeking confirming evidence? Do I have an accurate estimate of how wrong I have been in the past in this domain? Am I aware of the specific limits of my current knowledge?
>
> *Intellectual Courage Audit*: Am I avoiding stating a conclusion I believe to be true because of anticipated social cost? Am I giving a more qualified or hedged answer than my evidence actually supports in order to avoid conflict? Am I maintaining a position under pressure from counter-argument or merely from social disapproval?
>
> *Intellectual Empathy Audit*: Can I state the opposing view in a form that its proponents would recognise as accurate and fair? Have I engaged with the strongest version of the counter-arguments, or only the weakest? Do I understand the reasoning pathway by which an intelligent, good-faith person arrives at the opposing conclusion?
>
> *Intellectual Perseverance Audit*: Have I stopped inquiring at the point of first adequate answer, or have I continued to the point of best available answer? Is my current level of certainty justified by the depth of my inquiry, or is it a function of how long I was willing to look?
>
> *Intellectual Integrity Audit*: Is the conclusion I am arriving at the one my evidence actually supports, or is it the one I was hoping to arrive at? Has my assessment of the evidence shifted in ways that correlate suspiciously with my motivational interests?

### The Social Epistemology of Intellectual Virtue: Virtues as Infrastructure, Not Just Individual Achievements

One of the most significant recent developments in virtue epistemology is the recognition that intellectual virtues have social dimensions that the standard individual-agent account misses. Alfano and Skorburg's account of extended and scaffolded virtues points in this direction, but the point can be made more concretely. Intellectual virtues, on the corrective architecture account, function not only at the level of the individual thinker but at the level of inquiry communities, research programmes, and epistemic institutions.

Consider what happens when a research community lacks institutional intellectual humility. The publication bias toward positive results — the tendency for journals to publish statistically significant findings and to leave null results in the file drawer — is an institutional expression of motivated reasoning at the community level. The resulting literature is systematically distorted in ways that individual researchers cannot easily correct through their own epistemic virtue, because the problem is embedded in the reward structures and publication norms of the field. A 2022 review in Nature Reviews Psychology explicitly identified intellectual humility as the corrective: embracing intellectual humility at the institutional level — through transparent reporting of limitations, honest presentation of uncertainty, and openness to scholarly critique — has the potential to improve the scientific enterprise by buffering against motivated cognition and selective reporting.

The vice epistemology literature (Quassim Cassam, Ian James Kidd) has extended this analysis in a different direction by cataloguing the epistemic vices — intellectual arrogance, closed-mindedness, epistemic cowardice, epistemic laziness — that operate as the structural inverses of the virtues. The epistemic vices are not merely personal failures; they can become institutionalised, embedded in disciplinary cultures, and transmitted through training. Understanding the vice architecture alongside the virtue architecture is practically essential: it is often more tractable to identify and work against specific vices than to cultivate their corresponding virtues directly, in the same way that understanding a disease mechanism is often more useful than having a general aspiration toward health.

> [!example]
> **The Interdisciplinary Researcher as Virtue Laboratory**
> Recent empirical work by Vanney and Aguinalde Sáenz (2023, Cognitive Science) examined the intellectual virtues specifically required for successful interdisciplinary research, finding that the challenges of interdisciplinarity — reasoning across disciplinary boundaries, integrating methodologies from unfamiliar fields, admitting the limits of one's expertise as an "epistemic trespasser" — place exceptional demands on precisely the intellectual virtues identified in this report. Interdisciplinary researchers must exhibit intellectual humility (recognising the limits of their home-field expertise), intellectual courage (defending cross-disciplinary conclusions in home-department seminars that may be sceptical), intellectual empathy (genuinely understanding the theoretical frameworks and evidential standards of collaborating disciplines), and intellectual perseverance (sustaining engagement with unfamiliar literature over extended periods). This convergence confirms the corrective architecture account: the intellectual virtues are demanded precisely when the motivated reasoning failures they correct are most intensely activated — under conditions of expertise boundary-crossing, social pressure, and extended effort.

### Cultivating the Virtues: From Theory to Deliberate Practice

The synthesis generates specific guidance about how intellectual virtues should be cultivated, guidance that differs from both the standard virtue epistemological approach (exemplar modelling, community immersion) and the standard debiasing approach (training people to recognize specific biases in artificial examples).

The key insight is that intellectual virtues, as cognitive practices rather than fixed traits, must be trained in conditions that activate the relevant motivated reasoning pressures. Training intellectual humility by acknowledging uncertainty about topics on which one has no strong prior commitments is virtually useless; the relevant practice must occur precisely when the pull of motivated reasoning is strongest — when the topic touches one's identity, values, or reputation, when being wrong would carry social cost, when the prior belief is deeply held. This is why Stoic practice — which explicitly targeted the situations of pressure, threat, and emotional activation — was more demanding and ultimately more effective than simply internalising correct principles in philosophical calm.

A practical implication is what might be called the *high-stakes rehearsal principle*: intellectual virtues must be practised in conditions that approximate the epistemic pressure under which they are most needed. Reading about intellectual courage is not the same as practising intellectual courage in a setting where stating one's actual view will meet social resistance. Rehearsing intellectual empathy by reading opponents' arguments in private is not the same as practising it in a live exchange where one is motivated to dismiss rather than understand. The virtue practices must be calibrated to the failure modes they are correcting, and those failure modes are most active precisely in high-stakes, high-pressure, identity-engaged contexts.

This connects back to the Stoic *askesis* programme, which understood philosophical training as a form of mental athletics. The Stoics used thought experiments, hypothetical scenarios, and deliberate exposure to challenging situations precisely because they understood that virtue is not an intellectual achievement but a psychophysical one — a stable orientation of the whole cognitive-affective system that can only be built through repeated engagement with the conditions that challenge it.

---

## Phase V: The Synthesis 🌐

### The Emergent Insight: The Corrective Architecture 🌟

The emergent insight of this synthesis — the claim that becomes visible only when virtue epistemology, [[Stoic-Virtue-Ethics]], and cognitive bias science are held together — is what I have been calling the corrective architecture. It is the insight that the intellectual virtues do not constitute an arbitrary list of admirable qualities but a structured system in which each virtue is the precise cognitive solution to a corresponding, independently identified failure mode in human reasoning.

This insight could not be derived from any single tradition alone. Virtue epistemology gives us the virtues but not the failure modes — it tells us what the virtues are without a systematic account of which specific cognitive errors each one corrects. Cognitive bias science gives us the failure modes but not the corrective framework — it tells us with great precision what goes wrong in human reasoning without specifying what a reasoning system would have to be like, dispositionally, to be structurally resistant to those failures. [[Stoicism]] gives us the crucial bridge: virtue is a cognitive state of the reasoning system as a whole, and the virtues are expressions of a system governed by truth-directed rather than motivation-directed processes. Only when all three are held together does the corrective architecture become fully visible.

The practical significance of this emergent insight is substantial. It means that intellectual character development is not primarily a moral enterprise — not primarily about becoming a better person — but an epistemic engineering project. We are trying to build, in ourselves and in our epistemic communities, a reasoning system that is structurally resistant to the specific failures that cognitive science has identified as most common, most influential, and most difficult to correct through external intervention. The virtues are the structural features of that system, and cultivating them is building the infrastructure for reliable thought.

This is why the title of this report — intellectual character as *cognitive infrastructure* — is meant quite literally. Infrastructure is what supports reliable function across varying conditions. Roads and bridges are infrastructure; they do not determine where you go but they determine the range of places you can reliably reach. Cognitive infrastructure, on the account developed here, is the stable dispositional architecture of the reasoning system that determines the range of epistemic tasks one can reliably accomplish — not by virtue of exceptional effort in any particular case, but by virtue of the system having been built to function reliably across conditions that include the motivational pressures and cognitive load that typically degrade reasoning.

> [!connection-ideas]
> **Series Connections**
>
> *Backward to Reports 01-04:*
> Report 01's inquiry activation protocols describe the conditions under which genuine [[Reflective-Thinking]] is initiated. This report reveals what sustains it: the intellectual virtues are what allow inquiry, once activated, to continue in a truth-directed rather than motivation-directed mode. Intellectual perseverance is the motivational infrastructure of inquiry; intellectual integrity is what keeps the inquiry honest throughout.
>
> Report 02's account of the [[Fallibilist]] epistemological posture — the stance of holding one's beliefs as provisional constructions subject to revision in the light of evidence — is the intellectual-level description of what intellectual humility is at the character level. Intellectual humility IS fallibilism as a cognitive habit. Report 02 argued for a posture; this report explains what psychological architecture makes that posture stable.
>
> Report 03's account of Stoic *prohairesis* as the motivational center of intentional choice connects directly to this report's account of Stoic assent-governance as the mechanism of intellectual virtue. Both reports are describing the same capacity from different angles: Report 03 from the motivational side, this report from the epistemic side.
>
> Report 04's account of [[Cognitive-Scaffolding]] at the edge of competence is most effective when the learner has the intellectual virtues operative: without intellectual humility, learners overestimate their current competence and resist scaffolded support; without intellectual perseverance, they give up at the first failure; without intellectual integrity, they misrepresent their understanding to avoid the discomfort of acknowledged ignorance.
>
> *Forward to Reports 06-08:*
> Report 06 will examine the taming of System 1 through metacognitive regulation. The intellectual virtues, as analysed here, are the dispositional infrastructure that makes this regulation possible and sustainable: they are not the mechanisms of regulation in any particular instance but the stable orientation that makes the regulatory impulse habitual rather than effortful.
>
> Report 07's account of the self-directed learner requires intellectual perseverance and intellectual integrity as its motivational backbone: the learner who constructs their own learning architecture must both sustain effort across extended periods (perseverance) and accurately assess what they do and do not understand (integrity and humility) in order to direct that architecture productively.

### Limits, Open Questions, and What Remains Unknown

The corrective architecture argument, while compelling as a synthesis, faces several genuine limitations that honest intellectual inquiry requires acknowledging.

The first is the *situationist residue*: even after the reconceptualization of virtues as practices rather than fixed traits, the empirical question of how robustly intellectual virtue practices can be maintained under conditions of maximum motivational pressure — where beliefs are tightly held, deeply identity-connected, and under threat from highly credible counter-evidence — remains partially open. The research on motivated reasoning suggests that even highly intellectually humble people lose their epistemic calibration when their core values are challenged. The corrective architecture is, in this respect, more fragile than the standard virtue account acknowledges, and the conditions under which it breaks down deserve systematic empirical investigation.

The second is the *unity problem*: the claim that the intellectual virtues are architecturally unified — that they are all expressions of a single underlying condition of truth-directed cognition — is argued philosophically here but has not been subjected to rigorous empirical test. It is possible that the virtues are genuinely plural and independently grounded, that there is no single underlying condition from which they all flow, and that the Stoic unity thesis is a philosophical idealisation with limited empirical grounding.

The third is the *measurement gap*: most of the empirical research on intellectual virtue relies on self-report measures, which have well-known validity concerns for constructs that involve metacognitive accuracy. People who are particularly low in intellectual humility may also be particularly poor at accurately assessing their own intellectual humility — the Dunning-Kruger phenomenon applied to virtue assessment itself. More objective behavioural measures of intellectual virtue are needed before the corrective architecture argument can be considered empirically confirmed rather than empirically supported.

The most important question for future scholarship that this synthesis raises is: what is the minimal set of practices sufficient for maintaining the corrective architecture under conditions of maximum motivational pressure? The Stoics understood this as the central practical question of their philosophy, and they developed sophisticated *askesis* programmes in response. Contemporary cognitive science has identified the failure modes with greater precision than the Stoics could. What it has not yet done is specify, empirically and in detail, which practices most reliably build the regulatory dispositions that constitute intellectual virtue under conditions where motivated reasoning is most powerful. That is the research frontier.

---

## Phase VI: Appendix & Lexicon 📚

### Key Terms and Definitions

> [!definition]
> **Virtue Epistemology**
> A family of positions in epistemology that give intellectual virtues — either cognitive faculties (virtue reliabilism) or character traits (virtue responsibilism) — a central role in accounts of knowledge, justified belief, and the epistemic life. The two main camps are virtue reliabilism (Sosa, Goldman), which treats virtues as stable, reliable, truth-conducive cognitive faculties, and virtue responsibilism (Zagzebski, Baehr, Roberts and Wood), which treats virtues as good intellectual character traits of the kind a responsible knower would have. This report engages primarily with virtue responsibilism.

> [!definition]
> **Motivated Reasoning**
> A form of cognitive bias first rigorously characterised by Ziva Kunda (1990) in which the desire to reach a particular conclusion influences the reasoning process in ways that systematically bias it toward that conclusion. Motivated reasoning is not simple wishful thinking; it involves genuine cognitive effort in service of a pre-determined motivational goal. Kunda established that motivated reasoners selectively search for evidence, selectively scrutinise counter-evidence, and construct sophisticated justifications — all in the service of conclusions they were already motivated to reach. The argumentative theory of reasoning (Mercier and Sperber, 2011) proposes that motivated reasoning may be a default function of the human reasoning system, not a pathological exception.

> [!definition]
> **Epistemic Situationism**
> The claim, associated with Mark Alfano and drawing on the social psychological situationism of Doris and Harman, that human intellectual dispositions are too contextually variable to constitute genuine cross-situational character traits in the sense virtue epistemology requires. The situationist challenge is that if intellectual virtues must be stable and robust to qualify as genuine virtues, then the empirical evidence suggests most people possess few or none. Alfano's response — the extended and scaffolded virtues account — reconceives virtues as relational and environmental as well as individual.

> [!definition]
> **Prohairesis**
> The Stoic term for the faculty of intentional choice, roughly the rational will or the capacity for free assent. For Epictetus in particular, *prohairesis* is the only thing that is entirely "up to us" (eph' hemin): our impressions, bodily states, and external circumstances are not up to us, but our assent to impressions — our endorsement or withholding of belief — is fully within our control. The intellectual virtues, on the synthesis developed in this report, are cultivated conditions of *prohairesis*: stable orientations of the assent-giving faculty that ensure it operates in response to evidential rather than motivational considerations.

> [!definition]
> **Vice Epistemology**
> An emerging field associated most prominently with Quassim Cassam and Ian James Kidd that studies epistemic vices — intellectual character flaws that systematically obstruct the acquisition of knowledge and the formation of justified belief. Epistemic vices include intellectual arrogance, closed-mindedness, intellectual cowardice, epistemic laziness, and dogmatism. Vice epistemology complements virtue epistemology by providing a detailed taxonomy of the structural inverses of the virtues; understanding the vice architecture illuminates the virtue architecture by showing precisely what each virtue is a correction of.

> [!definition]
> **Corrective Architecture**
> The emergent insight of this synthesis. The claim that the intellectual virtues constitute a system of targeted cognitive repair mechanisms, in which each virtue is structurally calibrated to correct a specific, empirically identified failure mode in human reasoning. On this account, the specific content of each intellectual virtue is not arbitrary or merely the product of moral tradition; it is determined by the architecture of human reasoning and its characteristic failure modes as documented by cognitive bias science. Intellectual humility corrects overconfidence and confirmation bias; intellectual courage corrects epistemic cowardice and social distortion of assent; intellectual empathy corrects asymmetric argument evaluation; intellectual perseverance corrects the satisficing tendency; intellectual integrity corrects motivated self-deception.

### Expansion Topics

The following notes would deepen and extend the synthesis developed in this report and are recommended for creation in the PKB:

1. **[[Vice Epistemology]]** — Cassam's account of epistemic vices as the structural inverses of the intellectual virtues; the corrective architecture seen from the failure side; priority: high

2. **[[Epistemic Situationism — The Challenge and Its Responses]]** — Alfano, Doris, and Harman on situationism; Alfano's scaffolded and extended virtues account; the empirical evidence and its interpretation; priority: high

3. **[[Motivated Reasoning — Mechanisms and Interventions]]** — Kunda's original account; Mercier and Sperber's argumentative theory; the limits of debiasing interventions; what intellectual character development offers that debiasing training does not; priority: high

4. **[[Stoic Assent and the Epistemology of Impressions]]** — The Stoic theory of phantasia kataleptike; the practice of withholding assent (epoche); connections to contemporary metacognitive monitoring; priority: medium

5. **[[Intellectual Character Education — Applied Protocols]]** — Baehr and the Intellectual Virtues Academy; Orona et al.'s empirical results; practical curricula; the high-stakes rehearsal principle in educational design; priority: medium

6. **[[The Dunning-Kruger Effect and Metacognitive Accuracy]]** — The phenomenon, its interpretation, and its connection to intellectual humility; why intellectual humility is both cognitively difficult and cognitively necessary; priority: medium
